| [Jill Seymour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jill_Seymour "Jill Seymour") | [West Midlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Midlands_(European_Parliament_constituency) "West Midlands (European Parliament constituency)") | 1 July 2014 | 15April2019(2019-04-15) |
| [Jane Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jane_Collins "Jane Collins") | [Yorkshire and the Humber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yorkshire_and_the_Humber_(European_Parliament_constituency) "Yorkshire and the Humber (European Parliament constituency)") | 1 July 2014 | 15April2019(2019-04-15) |
| [Margot Parker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margot_Parker "Margot Parker") | [East Midlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Midlands_(European_Parliament_constituency) "East Midlands (European Parliament constituency)") | 1 July 2014 | 15April2019(2019-04-15) |
| [Jonathan Arnott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Arnott "Jonathan Arnott") | [North East England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_East_England_(European_Parliament_constituency) "North East England (European Parliament constituency)") | 1 July 2014 | 17April2019(2019-04-17) |
| [Ray Finch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ray_Finch "Ray Finch") | [South East England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_East_England_(European_Parliament_constituency) "South East England (European Parliament constituency)") | 1 July 2014 | 17April2019(2019-04-17) |

MEPs who joined the Brexit Party after foundation

Only three of these incumbent MEPs − Farage, Gill and Bullock − were selected to stand for the Brexit Party in the 2019 election,[&#8202;](#ref-back-212-0)[\[211\]](#refnote-212) which took place on 23 May 2019. Twenty-nine Brexit Party MEPs were elected to the [European Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Parliament "European Parliament"), including Tice and the former Conservative MP [Ann Widdecombe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ann_Widdecombe "Ann Widdecombe"), while Farage, [Nathan Gill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathan_Gill "Nathan Gill") and [Jonathan Bullock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Bullock "Jonathan Bullock") kept their seats.[&#8202;](#ref-back-213-0)[\[212\]](#refnote-213) BBC News described the Brexit Party, which gained 31.6% of the vote in the UK, as "the clear winner in the UK's European elections."[&#8202;](#ref-back-214-0)[\[213\]](#refnote-214)

The Brexit Party MEPs were not members of a group in the Parliament. The MEP [Andrew England Kerr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_England_Kerr "Andrew England Kerr") was expelled from the party on 29 September 2019 over a potential conflict of interest. Farage explained that England Kerr made "comments about a business and a product that he has a direct financial investment in and we think that is unacceptable."[&#8202;](#ref-back-215-0)[\[214\]](#refnote-215) The MEP [Louis Stedman-Bryce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Stedman-Bryce "Louis Stedman-Bryce") resigned on 19 November 2019 in response to "The Brexit Party's recent decision to select a Scottish candidate who has openly posted homophobic views".[&#8202;](#ref-back-216-0)[\[215\]](#refnote-216)

### London Assembly

In the [2024 London Assembly election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_London_Assembly_election "2024 London Assembly election"), [Alex Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_Wilson_(British_politician) "Alex Wilson (British politician)") was elected as a London-wide member for the [London Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Assembly "London Assembly").[&#8202;](#ref-back-London_Elects_2024-1)[\[60\]](#refnote-London_Elects_2024)

On 4 October 2025, [Keith Prince](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keith_Prince "Keith Prince"), the [Conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK) "Conservative Party (UK)") London Assembly member for [Havering and Redbridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havering_and_Redbridge_(London_Assembly_constituency) "Havering and Redbridge (London Assembly constituency)"), defected to Reform.[&#8202;](#ref-back-217-0)[\[216\]](#refnote-217)

### Senedd

See also: [Reform UK Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_UK_Wales "Reform UK Wales")

On 15 May 2019, four Members originally elected or co-opted for UKIP ( [Caroline Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caroline_Jones_(politician) "Caroline Jones (politician)"), [Mandy Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandy_Jones_(politician) "Mandy Jones (politician)"), [David Rowlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Rowlands_(politician) "David Rowlands (politician)") and [Mark Reckless](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Reckless "Mark Reckless")) joined the Brexit Party,[&#8202;](#ref-back-218-0)[\[217\]](#refnote-218) with Reckless being appointed as leader of their group,[&#8202;](#ref-back-219-0)[\[218\]](#refnote-219) which was known as _Plaid Brexit_ in [Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_language "Welsh language").[&#8202;](#ref-back-220-0)[\[219\]](#refnote-220) In May 2020 Reckless said that Nigel Farage is "consulted over key decisions... but he doesn't micro-manage us here", and that in the [2021 Senedd election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Senedd_election "2021 Senedd election") it would campaign to scrap the current system of devolution and replace it with a directly elected first minister accountable to Welsh MPs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-221-0)[\[220\]](#refnote-221) This policy announcement triggered the departure from the party's group in the Senedd of Caroline Jones, Mandy Jones and David Rowlands. They formed a new members group, the [Independent Alliance for Reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Alliance_for_Reform "Independent Alliance for Reform"), which sought to reform rather than abolish the Senedd.[&#8202;](#ref-back-222-0)[\[221\]](#refnote-222)[&#8202;](#ref-back-223-0)[\[222\]](#refnote-223) The remaining Brexit Party Senedd group member, Mark Reckless, left to join the [Abolish the Welsh Assembly Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolish_the_Welsh_Assembly_Party "Abolish the Welsh Assembly Party").[&#8202;](#ref-back-224-0)[\[223\]](#refnote-224)

Reform UK contested the [2021 Senedd election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Senedd_election "2021 Senedd election") on a platform of ending lockdowns, investing in the NHS, giving parents greater control over education, building the [M4 relief road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M4_relief_road "M4 relief road"), and cutting local government,[&#8202;](#ref-back-225-0)[\[224\]](#refnote-225) but did not win any seats, although they got a one percent vote share for regional and constituency lists.[&#8202;](#ref-back-226-0)[\[225\]](#refnote-226)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Dan_Thomas_AS_MS_%2855265691636%29.jpg/500px-Dan_Thomas_AS_MS_%2855265691636%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dan_Thomas_AS_MS_(55265691636).jpg) [Dan Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Thomas "Dan Thomas") with Reform UK [Members of the Senedd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_of_the_Senedd "Member of the Senedd").

On 22 July 2025, [Laura Anne Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laura_Anne_Jones "Laura Anne Jones"), then a [Welsh Conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_Conservatives "Welsh Conservatives") member of the Senedd for [South Wales East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Wales_East_(Senedd_electoral_region) "South Wales East (Senedd electoral region)"), defected to the party, becoming their first representative in the [Senedd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senedd "Senedd") since the party was renamed to Reform UK.[&#8202;](#ref-back-227-0)[\[226\]](#refnote-227) This decision came while she was under investigation by the Senedd standards watchdog due to reports of bullying and false expenses claims.[&#8202;](#ref-back-228-0)[\[227\]](#refnote-228)

On 20 January 2026, [James Evans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Evans_(Welsh_politician) "James Evans (Welsh politician)"), then a Welsh Conservative member of the Senedd for [Brecon and Randorshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brecon_and_Radnorshire_(Senedd_constituency) "Brecon and Radnorshire (Senedd constituency)"), had the whip removed following Evans confirming to [Darren Millar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darren_Millar "Darren Millar") that he was in talks with Reform UK about a possible defection.[&#8202;](#ref-back-229-0)[\[228\]](#refnote-229) Evans subsequently sat as an independent for 16 days before announcing his defection to Reform UK on 5 February 2026.[&#8202;](#ref-back-230-0)[\[229\]](#refnote-230)

On 7 May 2026, following the [2026 Senedd election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Senedd_election "2026 Senedd election"), Reform UK became the second largest party in the Senedd after [Plaid Cymru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaid_Cymru "Plaid Cymru"), winning 34 of the 96 seats up for election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-231-0)[\[230\]](#refnote-231) [Dan Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Thomas "Dan Thomas") subsequently became the [leader of the opposittion in Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Wales) "Leader of the Opposition (Wales)") and formed the [Thomas shadow cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_shadow_cabinet "Thomas shadow cabinet").[&#8202;](#ref-back-232-0)[\[231\]](#refnote-232)

On 15 September 2026, Dan Thomas resigned as leader of Reform UK Wales after being arrested on suspicion of assault and controlling or coercive behavior. Thomas said that he had "done nothing wrong". [Gwent Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwent_Police "Gwent Police") confirmed that a 45-year-old man from [Aberbargoed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aberbargoed "Aberbargoed") had been [released on bail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bail "Bail").[&#8202;](#ref-back-233-0)[\[232\]](#refnote-233) That same day, [Sarah Cooper-Lesadd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Cooper-Lesadd "Sarah Cooper-Lesadd"), then a Reform UK Wales member of the Senedd for [Pen-y-bont Bro Morgannwg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pen-y-bont_Bro_Morgannwg "Pen-y-bont Bro Morgannwg"), defected to Plaid Cymru.[&#8202;](#ref-back-234-0)[\[233\]](#refnote-234)

On 21 September 2026, [Helen Jenner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helen_Jenner "Helen Jenner") was unveiled as the successor to Dan Thomas following a [leadership contest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Reform_UK_Wales_leadership_election "2026 Reform UK Wales leadership election") by [Jason O'Connell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jason_O'Connell "Jason O'Connell"). Jenner had previously served as Thomas' [deputy leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deputy_Leader_of_Reform_UK_Wales "Deputy Leader of Reform UK Wales").[&#8202;](#ref-back-235-0)[\[234\]](#refnote-235) The next day, [Laura Anne Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laura_Anne_Jones "Laura Anne Jones") was announced as Jenner's deputy leader.[&#8202;](#ref-back-236-0)[\[235\]](#refnote-236)

### Scottish Parliament

See also: [Reform UK Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_UK_Scotland "Reform UK Scotland")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/f0/Malcolm_Offord_Official_MSP_portrait_2026_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Malcolm_Offord_Official_MSP_portrait_2026_(cropped).jpg) [Malcolm Offord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_Offord "Malcolm Offord") leads Reform UK in Scotland

On 11 January 2021, [Michelle Ballantyne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Ballantyne "Michelle Ballantyne"), an MSP for [South Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Scotland_(Scottish_Parliament_electoral_region) "South Scotland (Scottish Parliament electoral region)"), joined Reform UK. She first sat as a Conservative but left the party in 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-237-0)[\[236\]](#refnote-237)[&#8202;](#ref-back-238-0)[\[237\]](#refnote-238) Ballantyne continued to sit with the party until the [2021 Scottish Parliament election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Scottish_Parliament_election "2021 Scottish Parliament election") in May, when she lost her seat to a candidate from the [Scottish Conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Conservatives "Scottish Conservatives"). She resigned as the party's leader in Scotland in February 2022.[&#8202;](#ref-back-National_Feb_2022-2)[\[34\]](#refnote-National_Feb_2022)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Herald_Feb_2022-2)[\[35\]](#refnote-Herald_Feb_2022)

On 27 August 2025, [Graham Simpson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graham_Simpson_(politician) "Graham Simpson (politician)"), an MSP for [Central Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Scotland_(Scottish_Parliament_electoral_region) "Central Scotland (Scottish Parliament electoral region)"), defected from the Conservatives and joined Reform.[&#8202;](#ref-back-239-0)[\[238\]](#refnote-239)

On 7 May 2026, in the [2026 Scottish Parliament election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Scottish_Parliament_election "2026 Scottish Parliament election"), Reform UK became the joint-second largest party in the Scottish Parliament, tied with [Scottish Labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Labour "Scottish Labour") and behind the [Scottish National Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_National_Party "Scottish National Party"), with 17 of the 129 seats up for election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-240-0)[\[239\]](#refnote-240)

On 19 October 2026, [Amanda Bland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanda_Bland "Amanda Bland") resigned from Reform UK to become an independent, resulting in the party becoming the third largest in the parliament, holding 16 seats.[&#8202;](#ref-back-241-0)[\[240\]](#refnote-241)

### Police and crime commissioners

On 4 August 2025, [Rupert Matthews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert_Matthews "Rupert Matthews"), the [Leicestershire Police and Crime Commissioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leicestershire_Police_and_Crime_Commissioner "Leicestershire Police and Crime Commissioner") and a former MEP for [East Midlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Midlands_(European_Parliament_constituency) "East Midlands (European Parliament constituency)"), defected from the Conservatives and joined Reform.[&#8202;](#ref-back-242-0)[\[241\]](#refnote-242)

In July 2026, Reform candidate [Colin Sutton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colin_Sutton_(police_and_crime_commissioner) "Colin Sutton (police and crime commissioner)") won the [2026 Norfolk Police and Crime Commissioner by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Norfolk_Police_and_Crime_Commissioner_by-election "2026 Norfolk Police and Crime Commissioner by-election"), gaining the seat from Labour.[&#8202;](#ref-back-243-0)[\[242\]](#refnote-243)

### Local government

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/2025_United_Kingdom_local_elections_council_control.svg/500px-2025_United_Kingdom_local_elections_council_control.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:2025_United_Kingdom_local_elections_council_control.svg)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/2026_United_Kingdom_local_elections_council_control.svg/500px-2026_United_Kingdom_local_elections_council_control.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:2026_United_Kingdom_local_elections_council_control.svg)

Map of council control after the United Kingdom local elections in [2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_United_Kingdom_local_elections "2025 United Kingdom local elections") (left) and [2026](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_Kingdom_local_elections "2026 United Kingdom local elections") (right). Reform UK in light blue

As of March 2024, two-thirds of Reform UK's local councillors were former councillors of the Conservative party who defected over to Reform UK.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Tory_to_Reform-0)[\[243\]](#refnote-Tory_to_Reform) In October 2024, Farage called on Conservative Party councillors to join Reform UK, saying that he was contacting over a thousand of them and that "a huge number of them genuinely agree with us and what we stand for".[&#8202;](#ref-back-245-0)[\[244\]](#refnote-245)

Reform won a by-election in the Marton ward of Blackpool Council on 3 October 2024, with its vote share rising from 9.5% (in the 2023 election) to 38.8%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-246-0)[\[245\]](#refnote-246) In October 2024, two [Scottish Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Conservatives "Scottish Conservatives") councillors, Mark Findlater and Laurie Carnie, serving on [Aberdeenshire Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aberdeenshire_Council "Aberdeenshire Council") defected to the party and became Reform UK's first local representatives in Scotland.[&#8202;](#ref-back-247-0)[\[246\]](#refnote-247)[&#8202;](#ref-back-248-0)[\[247\]](#refnote-248) As of 17 March 2025, prior to the local elections of that year, 15 of the 113 Reform councillors had been won through elections, with the remainder defecting from other parties, the majority of which were from the Conservatives.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc-c871wzx8r47o-1)[\[94\]](#refnote-bbc-c871wzx8r47o) In June 2025, a [Scottish Labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Labour "Scottish Labour") councillor, Jamie McGuire, from [Renfrewshire Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renfrewshire_Council "Renfrewshire Council") defected to Reform along with a third former Scottish Conservatives councillor, Duncan Massey, from Aberdeen.[&#8202;](#ref-back-249-0)[\[248\]](#refnote-249)[&#8202;](#ref-back-250-0)[\[249\]](#refnote-250)

Reform polled in first place and won 677 seats in the [2025 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_United_Kingdom_local_elections "2025 United Kingdom local elections"). Within six weeks the number of Reform councillors had fallen to 668 due to five suspensions from the party, and four newly appointed councillors stepping down from the role, which led to two by-election losses (one to the Conservatives and the other to the Lib Dems).[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc-durham-0)[\[250\]](#refnote-bbc-durham)[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc-conservative-win-0)[\[251\]](#refnote-bbc-conservative-win) Two further by-elections were scheduled for July and August 2025.[&#8202;](#ref-back-all-candidates-0)[\[252\]](#refnote-all-candidates)[&#8202;](#ref-back-durham-bye-0)[\[253\]](#refnote-durham-bye)

Within months of the election, issues emerged for Reform UK party groups on local authorities. On 18 October 2025, _[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian "The Guardian")_ released video footage of an online meeting of Reform UK councillors where [Linden Kemkaran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linden_Kemkaran "Linden Kemkaran"), leader of [Kent County Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kent_County_Council "Kent County Council"), swore at some of her colleagues who criticised her leadership.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Linden_Kemkaran_Suck_it_uP-0)[\[254\]](#refnote-Linden_Kemkaran_Suck_it_uP) After the leak she called those responsible cowards.[&#8202;](#ref-back-256-0)[\[255\]](#refnote-256) Four councillors were suspended as a result of the leak.[&#8202;](#ref-back-257-0)[\[256\]](#refnote-257) On [Cornwall Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornwall_Council "Cornwall Council"), the leader and deputy leader of the group resigned.[&#8202;](#ref-back-258-0)[\[257\]](#refnote-258) Other local authorities where elected councillors who have left include [City of Doncaster Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Doncaster_Council "City of Doncaster Council"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-259-0)[\[258\]](#refnote-259) [Northumberland County Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northumberland_County_Council "Northumberland County Council"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-260-0)[\[259\]](#refnote-260) [Nottinghamshire County Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nottinghamshire_County_Council "Nottinghamshire County Council"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-261-0)[\[260\]](#refnote-261) [Staffordshire County Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Staffordshire_County_Council "Staffordshire County Council"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-262-0)[\[261\]](#refnote-262) and [Shropshire Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shropshire_Council "Shropshire Council").[&#8202;](#ref-back-263-0)[\[262\]](#refnote-263)

Reform had the most councillors elected in the [2026 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_Kingdom_local_elections "2026 United Kingdom local elections"), gaining 1,451 seats.[&#8202;](#ref-back-264-0)[\[263\]](#refnote-264)

## Ideology and platform

Reform UK is a [radical right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_right_(Europe) "Radical right (Europe)")[&#8202;](#ref-back-Webb2024-0)[\[264\]](#refnote-Webb2024)[&#8202;](#ref-back-v704-0)[\[265\]](#refnote-v704)[&#8202;](#ref-back-h440-0)[\[266\]](#refnote-h440) and [anti-immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_to_immigration "Opposition to immigration") political party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-i025-0)[\[267\]](#refnote-i025)[&#8202;](#ref-back-d8982-0)[\[268\]](#refnote-d8982) It is [right-wing populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-wing_populist "Right-wing populist")[&#8202;](#ref-back-populist-0)[\[b\]](#refnote-populist)[&#8202;](#ref-back-TournierSol2021-0)[\[271\]](#refnote-TournierSol2021)[&#8202;](#ref-back-CombeiReggi2024-0)[\[272\]](#refnote-CombeiReggi2024)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ColeStaffordHeinz2022-0)[\[273\]](#refnote-ColeStaffordHeinz2022) and [far-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_politics "Far-right politics").[&#8202;](#ref-back-hard-right-0)[\[c\]](#refnote-hard-right)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Miori-0)[\[277\]](#refnote-Miori)[&#8202;](#ref-back-281-0)[\[278\]](#refnote-281)[&#8202;](#ref-back-r302-0)[\[279\]](#refnote-r302) It is also [neoliberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoliberal "Neoliberal") populist,[&#8202;](#ref-back-283-0)[\[280\]](#refnote-283) [right-wing nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-wing_nationalist "Right-wing nationalist"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lockard2020-0)[\[281\]](#refnote-Lockard2020) and [national conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_conservatism "National conservatism").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-0)[\[282\]](#refnote-:3) Reform UK rejects the descriptor of far-right, threatening legal action against media using it,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Naughtie_2024_h130-0)[\[283\]](#refnote-Naughtie_2024_h130) while Farage has personally described Reform as occupying the "genuine [centre-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-right_politics "Centre-right politics")" and as a "brand new Conservative movement".[&#8202;](#ref-back-287-0)[\[284\]](#refnote-287)[&#8202;](#ref-back-288-0)[\[285\]](#refnote-288)[&#8202;](#ref-back-289-0)[\[286\]](#refnote-289)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-1)[\[67\]](#refnote-:7)

In March 2024, the [BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC "BBC") called the party far-right but soon retracted its statement and apologised to Reform UK, writing that describing the party as far-right "fell short of our usual editorial standards".[&#8202;](#ref-back-290-0)[\[287\]](#refnote-290) Commenting on the incident, professor of politics [Tim Bale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Bale "Tim Bale") wrote that labelling Reform UK as far-right is unhelpful, and that it "causes too visceral a reaction and at the same time is too broad to be meaningful", as an umbrella term. Bale stated that the importance of distinguishing between the " [extreme right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_right "Extreme right")" and "populist radical right", and stated that parties described as far right should instead be "more precisely labelled".[&#8202;](#ref-back-291-0)[\[288\]](#refnote-291) Bale later described party leader Farage as the "British representative of the populist radical right in Europe", while being placed "on the more moderate side of far-right parties in Europe."[&#8202;](#ref-back-292-0)[\[289\]](#refnote-292) In 2025 Bale stated that "most political scientists" would say that Reform is a far-right party, "if you accept that as the umbrella term and then say they're populist radical right".[&#8202;](#ref-back-293-0)[\[290\]](#refnote-293)

In _[Parliamentary Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_Affairs "Parliamentary Affairs")_, Bennie & Widfeldt described the party as fitting "into a broader European family of radical right parties working within democratic structures", with the radical right being a subset of far-right.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:13-0)[\[291\]](#refnote-:13) In [_British Politics_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Politics_(journal) "British Politics (journal)"), Hayton wrote that the party had evolved into a "fully-fledged right-wing populist party" that "can be most sensibly classified as a populist radical right party", having moved beyond the single-issue politics of Brexit while retaining a Eurosceptic legacy. Hayton placed this as "outside of the mainstream right and as part of the far right, but distinguishes it from the extreme right elements of the far right."[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-1)[\[282\]](#refnote-:3) In the _[Journal of Contemporary European Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_Contemporary_European_Studies "Journal of Contemporary European Studies")_, Shuttleworth, Brown & Mondon stated that the categorisation of Reform UK as far-right had been regularly left out from academic and public discourse, despite its "clear credentials", and questioned whether the BBC apology or the party's opposition to label was a reason for such exclusion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-295-0)[\[292\]](#refnote-295) Since the apology, the party has also been described as far-right in the _[Journal of European Public Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_European_Public_Policy "Journal of European Public Policy")_,[&#8202;](#ref-back-296-0)[\[293\]](#refnote-296) _[Journal of Cultural Economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_Cultural_Economy "Journal of Cultural Economy")_,[&#8202;](#ref-back-297-0)[\[294\]](#refnote-297) _[Journal of Elections, Public Opinion and Parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_Elections,_Public_Opinion_and_Parties "Journal of Elections, Public Opinion and Parties")_,[&#8202;](#ref-back-298-0)[\[295\]](#refnote-298) _[Ethnic and Racial Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_and_Racial_Studies "Ethnic and Racial Studies")_,[&#8202;](#ref-back-299-0)[\[296\]](#refnote-299) [_Nations and Nationalism_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nations_and_Nationalism_(journal) "Nations and Nationalism (journal)") _[&#8202;](#ref-back-:4-0)[\[297\]](#refnote-:4)_ and _[Studies in Conflict & Terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Studies_in_Conflict_&_Terrorism "Studies in Conflict & Terrorism")_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-301-0)[\[298\]](#refnote-301)

In the _[British Journal of Politics and International Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Journal_of_Politics_and_International_Relations "British Journal of Politics and International Relations")_, Newton categorised the party as right-wing populist that strategically utilises radical right populist ideology, differentiating it from minor [far-right parties in the UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_politics_in_the_United_Kingdom "Far-right politics in the United Kingdom") engaged in similar anti-migration activism.[&#8202;](#ref-back-302-0)[\[299\]](#refnote-302) Writing in _[The Political Quarterly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Political_Quarterly "The Political Quarterly")_, Wraight described Reform UK as having rallied voters behind their brand of " [little Englander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Englander "Little Englander") conservatism", as populist rivals to the right of the Conservative Party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-303-0)[\[300\]](#refnote-303) Writing in the magazine _[The Spectator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Spectator "The Spectator")_, [Ross Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ross_Clark_(journalist) "Ross Clark (journalist)") argued that Reform had become a left-wing party, by attracting disillusioned Labour voters with stances on restoring welfare benefits, nationalising the steel industry with 50% of utilities and increasing government spending (including the NHS).[&#8202;](#ref-back-304-0)[\[301\]](#refnote-304)

### 2019 European Parliament election platform as the Brexit Party

The party's constitution was published by the Electoral Commission as a result of a freedom of information request in May 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-305-0)[\[302\]](#refnote-305) It described the party as seeking to "promote and encourage those who aspire to improve their personal situation and those who seek to be self-reliant, whilst providing protection for those genuinely in need; favour the ability of individuals to make decisions in respect of themselves; seek to diminish the role of the State; lower the burden of taxation on individuals and businesses."[&#8202;](#ref-back-business-structure-0)[\[303\]](#refnote-business-structure)

[Social Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_(UK,_1990%E2%80%93present) "Social Democratic Party (UK, 1990–present)") politician [Patrick O'Flynn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_O'Flynn "Patrick O'Flynn"), who was elected as a UKIP MEP under Farage's leadership and supported the Brexit Party in the 2019 European elections, commented on the constitution's description of the party as following [classical liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_liberalism "Classical liberalism") and described them as having a [Thatcherite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thatcherism "Thatcherism") ideological core.[&#8202;](#ref-back-307-0)[\[304\]](#refnote-307) [James Glancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Glancy "James Glancy"), one of the party's MEPs, has compared the party to the [Referendum Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Referendum_Party "Referendum Party"), being a "united and diverse group of people from different political backgrounds".[&#8202;](#ref-back-308-0)[\[305\]](#refnote-308)

The party's first non-Brexit-related policy was announced on 4 June 2019: a proposition to transform [British Steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Steel_(2016%E2%80%93present) "British Steel (2016–present)") into a partly [worker-owned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worker_cooperative "Worker cooperative") company, in what was described as "a hybrid of Conservative and Labour policy".[&#8202;](#ref-back-309-0)[\[306\]](#refnote-309) The party also supported cutting Britain's foreign aid budget, scrapping the proposed [High Speed 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Speed_2 "High Speed 2") project and introducing free WiFi on all British public transport.[&#8202;](#ref-back-310-0)[\[307\]](#refnote-310)[&#8202;](#ref-back-311-0)[\[308\]](#refnote-311) The party also said it would scrap all interest paid on student tuition fees, reimburse graduates for historic interest payments made on their loans,[&#8202;](#ref-back-312-0)[\[309\]](#refnote-312) and pledged to abolish [inheritance tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inheritance_tax "Inheritance tax").[&#8202;](#ref-back-313-0)[\[310\]](#refnote-313)

In July 2019, the Brexit Party signed a cross-party declaration alongside the [Liberal Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Democrats_(UK) "Liberal Democrats (UK)"), [Green Party of England and Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Party_of_England_and_Wales "Green Party of England and Wales"), and the [Scottish National Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_National_Party "Scottish National Party"), calling for [first-past-the-post voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First-past-the-post_voting "First-past-the-post voting") to be replaced by a [proportional system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proportional_representation "Proportional representation") for Westminster elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-314-0)[\[311\]](#refnote-314)

### 2019 UK general election platform as the Brexit Party

On 22 November 2019, the Brexit Party set out its policy proposals for the [2019 UK general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election "2019 United Kingdom general election"). Its key policies for the election included:[&#8202;](#ref-back-BP-1)[\[24\]](#refnote-BP)[&#8202;](#ref-back-CP-1)[\[25\]](#refnote-CP)

- No extension to the [Brexit transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brexit_withdrawal_agreement "Brexit withdrawal agreement") period
- No privatisation of the [NHS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Health_Service "National Health Service")
- Reducing immigration
- Cutting [VAT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Value-added_tax_in_the_United_Kingdom "Value-added tax in the United Kingdom") on domestic fuel
- Banning the UK exporting its waste
- Providing free [broadband](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadband "Broadband") in deprived regions
- Scrapping the [television licence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_licensing_in_the_United_Kingdom "Television licensing in the United Kingdom") fee
- Abolishing [inheritance tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inheritance_tax_in_the_United_Kingdom "Inheritance tax in the United Kingdom")
- Scrapping [High Speed 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Speed_2 "High Speed 2") (HS2)
- Abolishing interest on [student loans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_loan "Student loan")
- Changing planning to help house building
- Reforming the [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_Kingdom "Supreme Court of the United Kingdom")
- [Reform the voting system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_reform "Electoral reform") to make it more representative
- Abolish the [House of Lords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Lords "House of Lords")
- Making MPs who switch parties subject to recall petitions
- Reform the postal voting system to combat fraud
- Introduce [citizens' initiatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_initiative "Popular initiative") to allow people to call [referendums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Referendums_in_the_United_Kingdom "Referendums in the United Kingdom"), subject to a 5 million threshold of registered voter signatures and time limitations on repeat votes

### 2020 to 2023 as Reform UK

Following the UK's departure from the European Union on 31 January 2020, Farage sought a new right-wing populist project for the party under its new name of Reform UK, opposing further COVID-19 restrictions, paralleling right-wing populist anti-lockdown sentiments in other countries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-315-0)[\[312\]](#refnote-315)

At the party conference in October 2021, leader Richard Tice criticised the Conservative Party as a party of "high tax". He said that his party would stand on a low-tax and low-regulation platform. The party supports raising the threshold at which people start paying income tax from £12,500 to £20,000, and exempting the smallest businesses from corporation tax. He has said that energy companies should be owned by the government or British pension funds to stop profits going abroad.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc-tice-attacks-0)[\[313\]](#refnote-bbc-tice-attacks)

In January 2023, Reform called for an end to foreign ownership of critical national infrastructure such as water, though as part of its plans private firms would continue to supply and distribute water.[&#8202;](#ref-back-317-0)[\[314\]](#refnote-317)

### 2024 UK general election platform as Reform UK

On 17 June 2024, Reform UK launched their manifesto, [Our Contract with You](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Our_Contract_with_You "Our Contract with You"), which Farage presented during an interview. The key policy proposals included:

- Tax cuts, including: raising the minimum threshold of [income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_the_United_Kingdom "Taxation in the United Kingdom") to £20,000, raising the higher rate threshold from £50,271 to £70,000,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sky_News,_June_2024-0)[\[315\]](#refnote-Sky_News,_June_2024) abolishing [stamp duty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stamp_duty_in_the_United_Kingdom "Stamp duty in the United Kingdom") for properties below £750,000, and abolishing [taxes on inheritances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inheritance_tax_in_the_United_Kingdom "Inheritance tax in the United Kingdom") below £2million.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ITVrun-0)[\[316\]](#refnote-ITVrun)[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBCrun-0)[\[317\]](#refnote-BBCrun)
- Reducing legal immigration by freezing "non-essential" immigration, and eliminating [illegal immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_immigration "Illegal immigration") by ending the settlement of any illegal immigrants, sending back to France migrants who arrive on boats [crossing the English Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Channel_illegal_migrant_crossings_(2018%E2%80%93present) "English Channel illegal migrant crossings (2018–present)"). To encourage companies to employ British workers, they would raise employers [National Insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Insurance "National Insurance") to 20% for foreign workers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ITVrun-1)[\[316\]](#refnote-ITVrun)[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBCrun-1)[\[317\]](#refnote-BBCrun)
- Scrapping and rejecting [net zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Net-zero_emissions "Net-zero emissions") as "the greatest act of negligence". Reform UK wants to increase drilling for gas and oil, seeing their expansion as growth opportunities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sky_News,_Reform_UK_policies-0)[\[318\]](#refnote-Sky_News,_Reform_UK_policies) It would also "fast-track" clean [nuclear energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power "Nuclear power") and shale gas licences.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Daily_Telegraph,_Reform_UK_policies-0)[\[319\]](#refnote-Daily_Telegraph,_Reform_UK_policies) It pledges to support the environment with tree planting, recycling and fewer single use plastics.[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBCrun-2)[\[317\]](#refnote-BBCrun)[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBCrunS-0)[\[320\]](#refnote-BBCrunS)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LGCrun-0)[\[321\]](#refnote-LGCrun)
- Eradicating waiting lists within two years by giving the NHS an extra £17bn a year and increasing the use of the private sector in the NHS, giving tax breaks to nurses and doctors to increase their number, and other measures including less tax for private healthcare and insurance, offering vouchers for private healthcare and looking to France's insurance-based health model.[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBCrun-3)[\[317\]](#refnote-BBCrun)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ITVrun-2)[\[316\]](#refnote-ITVrun)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sky_News,_Reform_UK_policies-1)[\[318\]](#refnote-Sky_News,_Reform_UK_policies)
- Increasing the number of police officers by 40,000 in five years, "clamp down on all crime and antisocial behaviour", by instituting [zero tolerance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zero_tolerance "Zero tolerance") policing.[&#8202;](#ref-back-STDrun-0)[\[322\]](#refnote-STDrun)
- Introducing a "patriotic curriculum" in schools, such that, for example, where imperialism or slavery is covered, examples are also given of non-European instances. " [Transgender ideology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_ideology "Transgender ideology")" would be banned, no gender questioning, social transitioning or "pronoun swapping" would be allowed in schools, universities would have to offer two-year courses to reduce student debt.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ITVrun-3)[\[316\]](#refnote-ITVrun)[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBCrun-4)[\[317\]](#refnote-BBCrun) Scrap interest on [student loans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_loan "Student loan") and extend the loan capital repayment periods to 45 years.[&#8202;](#ref-back-326-0)[\[323\]](#refnote-326) Encouraging the use of private schools via a 20% tax relief on private schooling.[&#8202;](#ref-back-327-0)[\[324\]](#refnote-327)
- Increasing defence spending to 2.5% of GDP in three years, and then to 3% over the following three. 30,000 additional people would be [recruited to join the army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_recruitment "Military recruitment").[&#8202;](#ref-back-STDrun-1)[\[322\]](#refnote-STDrun)
- Focus on new rail and road infrastructure in coastal regions, Wales, the North and the Midlands.[&#8202;](#ref-back-328-0)[\[325\]](#refnote-328)[&#8202;](#ref-back-329-0)[\[326\]](#refnote-329) [Public utilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_utility "Public utility") and critical infrastructure would come under 50% [public ownership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_ownership "State ownership"), the other 50% being owned by UK [pension fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pension_fund "Pension fund").[&#8202;](#ref-back-LGCrun-1)[\[321\]](#refnote-LGCrun)[&#8202;](#ref-back-STDrun-2)[\[322\]](#refnote-STDrun)[&#8202;](#ref-back-330-0)[\[327\]](#refnote-330)
- Increasing the farming budget to £3bn, focus on small farms, bring young people into farming.[&#8202;](#ref-back-331-0)[\[328\]](#refnote-331)
- Stopping EU fleets taking British fishing quotas, ban massive supertrawlers, and other fisheries measures.[&#8202;](#ref-back-332-0)[\[329\]](#refnote-332)
- Replacing the existing second chamber, the [House of Lords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Lords "House of Lords"), with a more democratic smaller alternative, having a referendum on the replacement of [first-past-the-post voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First-past-the-post_voting "First-past-the-post voting") with a system of [proportional representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proportional_representation "Proportional representation").[&#8202;](#ref-back-333-0)[\[330\]](#refnote-333)
- Eliminating the [TV licence fee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_licensing_in_the_United_Kingdom "Television licensing in the United Kingdom").[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBCrun-5)[\[317\]](#refnote-BBCrun)
- Leaving the [European Convention on Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Convention_on_Human_Rights "European Convention on Human Rights").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sky_News,_Reform_UK_policies-2)[\[318\]](#refnote-Sky_News,_Reform_UK_policies)
- Immediately cutting the rate of corporation tax from 25% to 20% and then further reduce corporation tax to 15% in the third year of parliament.[&#8202;](#ref-back-IFS-0)[\[331\]](#refnote-IFS)
- £150 billion per year in spending reductions, including public services and working-age benefits.[&#8202;](#ref-back-IFS-1)[\[331\]](#refnote-IFS)

Reform UK said that the total cost of its manifesto would be £140billion but say that they would raise £150billion. According to Reform UK, this money would be raised from the scrapping of net zero subsidies, the ending of payments of interest on [quantitative easing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantitative_easing "Quantitative easing") reserves to banks, the halving of foreign aid, cuts to working age benefits and other public spending reductions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-IFS-2)[\[331\]](#refnote-IFS)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Daily_Telegraph,_Reform_UK_policies-1)[\[319\]](#refnote-Daily_Telegraph,_Reform_UK_policies)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sky_News,_June_2024-1)[\[315\]](#refnote-Sky_News,_June_2024) The party said that it would "cut bureaucracy \[&#8202;](#ref-back-LGCrun-2)[…\] without touching frontline services",[\[321\]](#refnote-LGCrun) while the [Institute for Fiscal Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_for_Fiscal_Studies "Institute for Fiscal Studies") said that the savings required "would almost certainly require substantial cuts to the quantity or quality of public services" and that the sums of the costs of tax cuts and spending increases and savings proposed "do not add up" and were based on "extremely optimistic assumptions".[&#8202;](#ref-back-335-0)[\[332\]](#refnote-335)[&#8202;](#ref-back-336-0)[\[333\]](#refnote-336)[&#8202;](#ref-back-IFS-3)[\[331\]](#refnote-IFS)

The party's manifesto was criticised in a 2024 article by John Pring as threatening the rights of disabled people and as posing significant safeguarding risks to benefit claimants.[&#8202;](#ref-back-337-0)[\[334\]](#refnote-337)\[ _[bettersourceneeded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Questionable_sources "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_\]

Analysis in 2024 found that Reform UK's tax plans disproportionately benefit high earners.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sky_News,_June_2024-2)[\[315\]](#refnote-Sky_News,_June_2024) Farage said that his plans to raise the minimum threshold at which workers start paying tax from £12,571 to £20,000, would result in millions of low-paid workers not paying tax altogether.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sky_News,_June_2024-3)[\[315\]](#refnote-Sky_News,_June_2024) Reform UK wants to raise the higher rate threshold of tax from £50,271 to £70,000, which would result in a tax cut of close to £6,000 for the top 10% of earners. According to analysis for [Sky News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sky_News "Sky News") this would far outweigh any benefit to the lowest earners.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sky_News,_June_2024-4)[\[315\]](#refnote-Sky_News,_June_2024)

### 2025

In April 2025, Reform called for the [nationalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalisation "Nationalisation") of the steel plant in Scunthorpe and government take-over of two electric arc furnaces at Liberty Steel's plant in Rotherham.[&#8202;](#ref-back-338-0)[\[335\]](#refnote-338)

In September 2025, Reform said they would abolish [indefinite leave to remain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indefinite_leave_to_remain "Indefinite leave to remain").[&#8202;](#ref-back-339-0)[\[336\]](#refnote-339) Reform also proposed significant reforms to disability benefits, primarily focused on reducing the welfare bill by tightening eligibility and reassessment processes, particularly for those with less severe or non-serious conditions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pring,_September_2025-0)[\[337\]](#refnote-Pring,_September_2025) The party aims to move over a million people back into work, suggesting that for those considered fit to work, benefits would be withdrawn if they decline two job offers or fail to find employment within four months. They argue that work itself can be beneficial for mental health.[&#8202;](#ref-back-341-0)[\[338\]](#refnote-341)

Reform UK has echoed Trump's "Make America Great Again" slogan, using "make Britain great again", and has set up its own [Department of Government Efficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Government_Efficiency "Department of Government Efficiency") (DOGE), mirroring the initiative by the second Trump administration to cut government spending, which as of 25 March 2025 was led by Elon Musk.[&#8202;](#ref-back-342-0)[\[339\]](#refnote-342)

Policies on immigration and asylum include: leaving the European Convention on Human Rights (ECHR); repealing the Human Rights Act 1998 and replacing it with a "British Bill of Rights"; disapplying the 1951 Refugee Convention, the UN Convention Against Torture, and the Council of Europe Anti-Trafficking Convention (ECAT); creating detention powers without Hardial Singh constraints; passing an illegal migration (mass deportation bill) and deny asylum for illegal immigrants.[&#8202;](#ref-back-343-0)[\[340\]](#refnote-343) Reform said in November it would remove the rights of EU citizens to claim benefits, which would save £6.4 billion for 2025 rising to £10 billion by the end of the decade.[&#8202;](#ref-back-344-0)[\[341\]](#refnote-344)

### Climate change

Further information: [Climate change in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_the_United_Kingdom "Climate change in the United Kingdom")

A November 2024 poll conducted by [YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov "YouGov") found that Reform UK voters are twice as likely as the general public to believe that climate change is not caused by human activity.[&#8202;](#ref-back-345-0)[\[342\]](#refnote-345)

A policy document named "Reform is Essential" published in late 2022 contained a subchapter named " _An honest debate on climate change_" in which it said among other things: "Reform UK fully accepts that climate change is real, after all it has happened for millions of years based on multiple factors completely outside human control or influence. Warming has of course taken place over the last approximately 150 years, with signs over recent years that it is now leveling off."[&#8202;](#ref-back-346-0)[\[343\]](#refnote-346) In July 2024, in the run-up to the general election, the party broadly opposed the UK's legally binding net‑zero commitments, repeatedly pledging to scrap them, and according to _[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian "The Guardian")_ many of its leading members had downplayed or denied climate science consensus.[&#8202;](#ref-back-347-0)[\[344\]](#refnote-347) This is a marked change in British politics which up until recently enjoyed cross-party consensus on achieving net zero.[&#8202;](#ref-back-348-0)[\[345\]](#refnote-348)[&#8202;](#ref-back-349-0)[\[346\]](#refnote-349)[&#8202;](#ref-back-350-0)[\[347\]](#refnote-350) Before the 2024 election and following it, the party attracted several donors associated with the [fossil fuel industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel_industry "Fossil fuel industry") or [climate scepticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_scepticism "Climate scepticism"), including David Lilley, Roger Nagioff and Fiona Cottrell.[&#8202;](#ref-back-i366-0)[\[348\]](#refnote-i366)[&#8202;](#ref-back-t870-0)[\[349\]](#refnote-t870)

In an interview with the BBC in October 2021, then party chairman, Richard Tice criticised the Conservative Party's plans to decarbonise the economy, saying: "It is not net zero – it is literally net stupid." Instead, he suggested that the UK should focus on exploiting reserves of [shale gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shale_gas "Shale gas").[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc-tice-attacks-1)[\[313\]](#refnote-bbc-tice-attacks) Tice repeated the same stance in April 2022 saying that the Conservative Party's climate policy was out of touch with their voters and that Reform wanted to "cut taxes, go for growth, become self-reliant and use our own shale gas."[&#8202;](#ref-back-tele-new-brexit-0)[\[350\]](#refnote-tele-new-brexit) Reform UK's 2024 election manifesto rejected net zero and encouraged the use of fossil fuels.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Daily_Telegraph,_Reform_UK_policies-2)[\[319\]](#refnote-Daily_Telegraph,_Reform_UK_policies)

In June 2024, Pallavi Sethi and [Bob Ward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Ward_(communications_director) "Bob Ward (communications director)") of the [Grantham Research Institute on Climate Change and the Environment (London School of Economics)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grantham_Research_Institute_on_Climate_Change_and_the_Environment_(London_School_of_Economics) "Grantham Research Institute on Climate Change and the Environment (London School of Economics)") at [London School of Economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_School_of_Economics "London School of Economics") accused Reform UK and its leaders Nigel Farage and Richard Tice of promoting misinformation about climate change.[&#8202;](#ref-back-354-0)[\[351\]](#refnote-354)

_The Guardian_ reported in June 2024 that climate change deniers and sceptics were donating to Reform and Nigel Farage in 2024. The donors with stakes in the fossil fuel industry who were said to have donated were [Terence Mordaunt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terence_Mordaunt "Terence Mordaunt") and [Jeremy Hosking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremy_Hosking "Jeremy Hosking"). Donors who had donated to both [The Global Warming Policy Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Global_Warming_Policy_Foundation "The Global Warming Policy Foundation") (GWPF) and Reform were [Jon Moynihan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jon_Moynihan "Jon Moynihan"), [Nigel Vinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Vinson "Nigel Vinson"), and [Michael Hintze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Hintze "Michael Hintze").[&#8202;](#ref-back-t870-1)[\[349\]](#refnote-t870)

In February 2025, Tice said: "There's no evidence that man-made CO2 is going to change climate change. Given that it's gone on for millions of years, it will go on for millions of years" and that there were "a thousand" scientists who agreed with him, which was "not a minority". Scientists told _Sky News_ that his first point was "categorically wrong", "missing the point" and that the "evidence is clear", regarding the second point, 99% of scientific publications agree with the scientific consensus.[&#8202;](#ref-back-355-0)[\[352\]](#refnote-355)

In March 2025, _[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times "The New York Times")_ reported: "Most of those bankrolling Reform in 2024 were multimillionaires, individuals and companies based in low-tax offshore countries, climate change sceptics, or those with investments in fossil fuels or other climate-polluting industries."[&#8202;](#ref-back-356-0)[\[353\]](#refnote-356)

In June 2024, Reform UK had accepted "more than £2.3 million from oil and gas interests, highly polluting industries, and 'climate science deniers' since December 2019".[&#8202;](#ref-back-357-0)[\[354\]](#refnote-357)[&#8202;](#ref-back-358-0)[\[355\]](#refnote-358)

In April 2025, Farage said that the current government's net zero policy was "lunacy" and that "this could be the next Brexit", "where Parliament is so hopelessly out of touch with the country.” In response, the [Secretary of State for Energy Security and Net Zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Energy_Security_and_Net_Zero "Secretary of State for Energy Security and Net Zero") [Ed Miliband](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ed_Miliband "Ed Miliband") accused him of peddling "nonsense and lies".[&#8202;](#ref-back-359-0)[\[356\]](#refnote-359)[&#8202;](#ref-back-360-0)[\[357\]](#refnote-360) In July 2025, Reform UK's [Mayor of Greater Lincolnshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Greater_Lincolnshire "Mayor of Greater Lincolnshire"), Andrea Jenkyns, said, "Do I believe that climate change exists? No."[&#8202;](#ref-back-361-0)[\[358\]](#refnote-361)

In July 2025, Richard Tice, Dame Andrea Jenkyns and Reform UK councillor Sean Matthews, who leads [Lincolnshire County Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincolnshire_County_Council "Lincolnshire County Council"), launched a campaign opposing wind and solar farms and battery storage facilities, with Tice saying the campaign would use "every lever available" to stop these developments.[&#8202;](#ref-back-362-0)[\[359\]](#refnote-362)

In November 2025, when asked by [Sky News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sky_News "Sky News") if humans have impacted the climate, Tice said: "Possibly, but if so, a very modest percentage." He accepted the need to update infrastructure in Britain to cope with a changing climate. When asked whether he accepted that the climate was warming at an unprecedented rate, Tice said: "From the data that I've seen, from previous ice core data, I think the answer to that is questionable." [NASA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASA "NASA") has stated "human activity is the principal cause" of unprecedentedly fast warming and 234 UN scientists from the [Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intergovernmental_Panel_on_Climate_Change "Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change") have said it is "unequivocal" that humans have caused "widespread and rapid changes".[&#8202;](#ref-back-363-0)[\[360\]](#refnote-363)

### 2026

In February 2026, Robert Jenrick said that Reform would reinstate the [two child benefit cap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benefit_cap "Benefit cap"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-364-0)[\[361\]](#refnote-364) as part of an attempt to be more "fiscally responsible" in their policies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-0)[\[362\]](#refnote-:6) This came four months after [Rachel Reeves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel_Reeves "Rachel Reeves") announced that the benefit cap was to be abolished,[&#8202;](#ref-back-366-0)[\[363\]](#refnote-366) and was a change of policy, it previously having been to remove it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-1)[\[362\]](#refnote-:6)[&#8202;](#ref-back-367-0)[\[364\]](#refnote-367)

In 2026, Reform published draft legislation, _Cryptoassets and Digital Finance Bill_,[&#8202;](#ref-back-reformparty/crypto-bill-0)[\[365\]](#refnote-reformparty/crypto-bill) since removed from the Reform UK website.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc/cg4w6wqye32o-0)[\[366\]](#refnote-bbc/cg4w6wqye32o)[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc/cg4vnd0d17ro-0)[\[367\]](#refnote-bbc/cg4vnd0d17ro)[&#8202;](#ref-back-standard/b1230382-0)[\[368\]](#refnote-standard/b1230382)

In August 2026 [Richard Tice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Tice "Richard Tice") said: "Climate change has always happened. It always will. Maybe the smarter thing to do is to adapt to it than think arrogantly that you can stop it. When it's a bit warm, let's enjoy it. And if it means that English wine and sparkling wine gets better and better, fantastic."\[ _[needs context](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Writing_better_articles#Provide_context_for_the_reader "Wikipedia:Writing better articles")_\][&#8202;](#ref-back-372-0)[\[369\]](#refnote-372)[&#8202;](#ref-back-373-0)[\[370\]](#refnote-373)

Also in August 2026, Reform UK proposed cutting £50 billion in welfare/benefits if it were in government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-50bn-0)[\[371\]](#refnote-50bn) It estimates 2.89 million people would have their disability payments' removed, or altered under plans to abolish [Personal Independence Payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_Independence_Payment "Personal Independence Payment") (Pip) for working age people. Reform UK proposed making companies pay for any employees off sick for the first two years.[&#8202;](#ref-back-50bn-1)[\[371\]](#refnote-50bn) A new system, known as a Health Security Allowance, would replace both Pip and the [Universal Credit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Credit "Universal Credit") health element.[&#8202;](#ref-back-50bn-2)[\[371\]](#refnote-50bn)

### Foreign policy

Farage supported Israeli airstrikes against Iran during the [Twelve-Day War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day_War "Twelve-Day War") in 2025, arguing Israel had "no choice" in the situation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-375-0)[\[372\]](#refnote-375)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-0)[\[373\]](#refnote-:2) In June 2025, Farage said he was opposed to "de-escalation" with Iran and instead wanted to see its "bloody awful regime" be "wiped out".[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-1)[\[373\]](#refnote-:2)

Farage initially supported Israel and the US in the [2026 Iran war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Iran_war "2026 Iran war"). He criticised the decision by the prime minister, Keir Starmer, for the UK to [not be involved in offensive strikes against Iran and to deny the USA access](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premiership_of_Keir_Starmer#Iran "Premiership of Keir Starmer") to UK military bases to enable them. In response, Farage said "The Prime Minister needs to change his mind on the use of our military bases and back the Americans in this vital fight against Iran!"[&#8202;](#ref-back-377-0)[\[374\]](#refnote-377)[&#8202;](#ref-back-378-0)[\[375\]](#refnote-378)[&#8202;](#ref-back-379-0)[\[376\]](#refnote-379) He also stated his support for the US achieving [regime change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regime_change "Regime change") in Iran,[&#8202;](#ref-back-380-0)[\[377\]](#refnote-380) one of their stated objectives for the conflict.[&#8202;](#ref-back-381-0)[\[378\]](#refnote-381)[&#8202;](#ref-back-382-0)[\[379\]](#refnote-382) However, shortly after, Farage reversed his position to say Britain should not get "involved in another foreign war",[&#8202;](#ref-back-383-0)[\[380\]](#refnote-383) while Jenrick said that Reform's position was that "this war needs to come to an end as soon as possible because it is making Britain poorer", criticising the "liberal interventionists of the early 21st century".[&#8202;](#ref-back-384-0)[\[381\]](#refnote-384)

## Voting history

In October 2024, all Reform UK MPs voted against the [Employment Rights Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Employment_Rights_Act_2025 "Employment Rights Act 2025").[&#8202;](#ref-back-388-0)[\[385\]](#refnote-388) A policy within the bill is workers' prevention from harassment, which has been heavily criticised by Farage and other Reform UK politicians, who have referred to it as a "banter ban".[&#8202;](#ref-back-ERB-0)[\[386\]](#refnote-ERB) The general secretary of the [Trades Union Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trades_Union_Congress "Trades Union Congress") (TUC), [Paul Nowak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Nowak_(trade_unionist) "Paul Nowak (trade unionist)"), said on 28 April 2025, "The likes of Reform are defying their supporters by voting against improvements to workers' rights at every stage."[&#8202;](#ref-back-ERB-1)[\[386\]](#refnote-ERB)

In January 2025, all Reform UK MPs voted for an amendment to the [Children's Wellbeing and Schools Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Children's_Wellbeing_and_Schools_Bill "Children's Wellbeing and Schools Bill") regarding a new national inquiry into grooming gangs. The amendment was intended to block the bill and its passing would have halted the bill's progress in Parliament.[&#8202;](#ref-back-390-0)[\[387\]](#refnote-390) The Children's Wellbeing and Schools Bill aims to improve laws regarding home-schooling and support for children in care, improve school inspections and improve safeguards regarding private education facilities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-391-0)[\[388\]](#refnote-391) The amendment to the bill was lost by 364 votes to 111, a majority of 253 against the amendment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-397-0)[\[394\]](#refnote-397) Farage stated Reform UK will launch their own independent inquiry and pay for it themselves as the government one was rejected in order to respond to the "overwhelming demand" of the public to know the "full, unvarnished truth". Farage said the attacks were racist against white children by Pakistani rapists. Farage also criticised the Conservatives saying, "Talk is cheap. The Conservatives had 14 years in government to launch an inquiry. The establishment has failed the victims of grooming gangs on every level."[&#8202;](#ref-back-398-0)[\[395\]](#refnote-398)[&#8202;](#ref-back-399-0)[\[396\]](#refnote-399)

Of Reform UK's 8 MPs, 5 voted against the [Terminally Ill Adults (End of Life) Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terminally_Ill_Adults_(End_of_Life)_Bill "Terminally Ill Adults (End of Life) Bill") and the other 3 did not vote.[&#8202;](#ref-back-400-0)[\[397\]](#refnote-400)

## Funding and structure

Further information: [Reform UK financial allegations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_UK_financial_allegations "Reform UK financial allegations")

In its early days, the Brexit Party officially had three members, who were Farage, Tracey Knowles and Mehrtash A'Zami. The party opted for signing up registered supporters rather than members. The party structure was criticised for not providing the party's over 115,000 paying registered supporters[&#8202;](#ref-back-401-0)[\[398\]](#refnote-401) with any voting power to influence party policy;[&#8202;](#ref-back-business-structure-1)[\[303\]](#refnote-business-structure) Farage retained a high level of control over decision-making, including hand-picking candidates himself.[&#8202;](#ref-back-business-structure-2)[\[303\]](#refnote-business-structure)[&#8202;](#ref-back-402-0)[\[399\]](#refnote-402) Since 2021, the party has options to become a member, rather than a supporter.[&#8202;](#ref-back-403-0)[\[400\]](#refnote-403)

Initially the Reform UK party was a limited company (the Reform UK Party Limited)[&#8202;](#ref-back-INDY-0)[\[401\]](#refnote-INDY) with fifteen [shares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Share_(finance) "Share (finance)"). Farage owned 53% of the shares in the company, giving him a controlling majority. The other shareholders were Tice, who holds about a third, and Chief Executive Paul Oakden and Party Treasurer Mehrtash A'Zami who each held less than seven percent.[&#8202;](#ref-back-INDY-1)[\[401\]](#refnote-INDY) In August 2024 Paul Oakden was removed and Farage took over his shares, giving him 60% ownership.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Walker-0)[\[402\]](#refnote-Walker)As of 2025[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Reform_UK&action=edit), ownership of the party was transferred from Farage to a new business legally constituted as Reform 2025 Limited, a [company limited by guarantee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_limited_by_guarantee "Company limited by guarantee"), replacing the original company which was controlled by Farage as majority shareholder.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NF-0)[\[403\]](#refnote-NF)[&#8202;](#ref-back-DF-0)[\[404\]](#refnote-DF) The directors and guarantors of the new company are Farage and [Zia Yusuf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zia_Yusuf "Zia Yusuf"), who will effectively control the new company.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NF-1)[\[403\]](#refnote-NF)[&#8202;](#ref-back-DF-1)[\[404\]](#refnote-DF)[&#8202;](#ref-back-408-0)[\[405\]](#refnote-408) The business's filing stated that it had no "person with significant control".[&#8202;](#ref-back-NF-2)[\[403\]](#refnote-NF)

In 2019 Farage said the party would largely be funded by small donations and that they raised "£750,000 in donations online, all in small sums of less than £500" in their first ten days. The party also accepts large donations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Tele001-0)[\[406\]](#refnote-Tele001) He further said that the party would not be taking money from the key former UKIP funder [Arron Banks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arron_Banks "Arron Banks").[&#8202;](#ref-back-guardian-1)[\[19\]](#refnote-guardian)[&#8202;](#ref-back-guardian2-0)[\[407\]](#refnote-guardian2) Farage personally faced questions during the [2019 electoral campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election_in_the_United_Kingdom "2019 European Parliament election in the United Kingdom") after _[Channel 4 News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_4_News "Channel 4 News")_ revealed [undeclared travel and accommodation benefits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Farage#Arron_Banks_funding "Nigel Farage") provided by Banks before Farage joined the Brexit Party, and on 21 May 2019 the European Parliament formally opened an investigation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-411-0)[\[408\]](#refnote-411) In response to the reporting, the Brexit Party banned _Channel 4 News_ from its events.[&#8202;](#ref-back-412-0)[\[409\]](#refnote-412)

In 2019, £6.4m was donated to the party by [Christopher Harborne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Harborne "Christopher Harborne"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-eye1517-0)[\[410\]](#refnote-eye1517) and £200,000 by [Jeremy Hosking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremy_Hosking "Jeremy Hosking"), a former donor to the [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK) "Conservative Party (UK)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Tele001-1)[\[406\]](#refnote-Tele001) 2023 donations included £200,000 from [Terence Mordaunt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terence_Mordaunt "Terence Mordaunt")'s company First Corporate Consultants Ltd.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Allegretti_Willoughby_2024_i732-0)[\[411\]](#refnote-Allegretti_Willoughby_2024_i732)

Two days before the [2019 European election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election_in_the_United_Kingdom "2019 European Parliament election in the United Kingdom"), Farage accused the [Electoral Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Commission_(United_Kingdom) "Electoral Commission (United Kingdom)") of "interfering in the electoral process" after the independent watchdog visited the Brexit Party headquarters for "active oversight and regulation" of party funding.[&#8202;](#ref-back-415-0)[\[412\]](#refnote-415) Official donations of £500 or more must be given by a "permissible donor", who should either be somebody listed on the British electoral roll or a business registered at [Companies House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Companies_House "Companies House") and operating in Britain. When asked if the party took donations in foreign currency, Farage replied: "Absolutely not, we only take sterling – end of conversation." [Shadow Chancellor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer "Shadow Chancellor of the Exchequer") [John McDonnell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McDonnell "John McDonnell") called for "a full and open and transparent, independent inquiry into the funding of Mr Farage".[&#8202;](#ref-back-416-0)[\[413\]](#refnote-416) The Electoral Commission reported in July 2019 that following its visit it made recommendations to the party for more robust internal controls on permissible donations, as those in place had not been adequate, and that the party had returned a donation of £1,000 whose source could not be identified as acceptable.[&#8202;](#ref-back-417-0)[\[414\]](#refnote-417)

In May 2024, _[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian "The Guardian")_ said that 80% of the party's funding, in loans and donations, came from Tice. It reported Tice as saying that the Conservatives spend £35million annually, while Reform spends less than £1.5million.[&#8202;](#ref-back-418-0)[\[415\]](#refnote-418)

On 12 June 2025, [openDemocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OpenDemocracy "OpenDemocracy") reported that "Reform has received almost £5m from wealthy donors since 2023, including those with links to fossil fuels, the financial services industry and tax havens".[&#8202;](#ref-back-openDem-0)[\[416\]](#refnote-openDem) Around a quarter of donations have been from former Conservative Party donors and Reform UK has an unusually high number of overseas donors with connections to tax havens.[&#8202;](#ref-back-openDem-1)[\[416\]](#refnote-openDem)

In the first three months of 2026, [cryptocurrency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cryptocurrency "Cryptocurrency") tycoons, made several large donations to Reform UK: Harborne donated a further £3million, and [Ben Delo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Delo "Ben Delo"), billionaire co-founder of [BitMEX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BitMEX "BitMEX"), donated £4million.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Independent1-0)[\[417\]](#refnote-Independent1)

### Delo and Harborne record donations

On 11 September 2026, after allegations of illegal funding for Reform UK, Ben Delo donated a further £36million, the largest donation to a political party in British history to that date, beating the previous record of £10million to the Conservatives in 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:22-0)[\[418\]](#refnote-:22)[&#8202;](#ref-back-422-0)[\[419\]](#refnote-422)[&#8202;](#ref-back-423-0)[\[420\]](#refnote-423) The following day, Christopher Harborne matched Delo's £36million donation. The £72million in donations exceeded total donations to all UK political parties combined in 2025,[&#8202;](#ref-back-424-0)[\[421\]](#refnote-424) and was far in excess of the roughly £25million given to other parties in 2026 so far,[&#8202;](#ref-back-425-0)[\[422\]](#refnote-425) and the £43 million that Labour has received from trade unions since 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-426-0)[\[423\]](#refnote-426) Delo said that he made the donation because he wanted a "fair fight and a level playing field".[&#8202;](#ref-back-:22-1)[\[418\]](#refnote-:22)

The size of the donations led to calls from the [Liberal Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Democrats_(UK) "Liberal Democrats (UK)"), the [Green Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Party_of_England_and_Wales "Green Party of England and Wales"), and [Unite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unite_the_Union "Unite the Union") general secretary [Sharon Graham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharon_Graham "Sharon Graham") for a cap on political donations by a single donor.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:111-0)[\[424\]](#refnote-:111) Some MPs, including [Stella Creasy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stella_Creasy "Stella Creasy"), had made proposals for such changes earlier in the month.[&#8202;](#ref-back-428-0)[\[425\]](#refnote-428)[&#8202;](#ref-back-429-0)[\[426\]](#refnote-429) [Conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK) "Conservative Party (UK)") shadow foreign secretary [Tom Tugendhat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Tugendhat "Tom Tugendhat") warned of an "Americanisation of politics where money just sort of runs free".[&#8202;](#ref-back-:111-1)[\[424\]](#refnote-:111) Liberal Democrat spokesperson [Lisa Smart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisa_Smart "Lisa Smart") accused Nigel Farage of "buying his way into power".[&#8202;](#ref-back-:22-2)[\[418\]](#refnote-:22) [Trades Union Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trades_Union_Congress "Trades Union Congress") general secretary [Paul Nowak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Nowak_(trade_unionist) "Paul Nowak (trade unionist)") said the government must "stop the super-rich corrupting our politics".[&#8202;](#ref-back-:111-2)[\[424\]](#refnote-:111)[&#8202;](#ref-back-430-0)[\[427\]](#refnote-430) In a 14 September [YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov "YouGov") poll, 60% of respondents supported a cap on donations from individuals and entities, and 22% were opposed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-431-0)[\[428\]](#refnote-431)

Delo and Harborne had both lived outside the UK until earlier in 2026.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:111-3)[\[424\]](#refnote-:111) In March 2026, the government had announced legislation to cap donations from foreign residents at £100,000 per year, including for the first year after their return. The legislation was still in Parliament as of September 2026, but is due to apply retroactively to all donations made since its announcement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:111-4)[\[424\]](#refnote-:111)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:32-0)[\[429\]](#refnote-:32) Delo had said in April that he would return to the UK, and Harborne is believed to have registered his residence in June.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:32-1)[\[429\]](#refnote-:32)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:43-0)[\[430\]](#refnote-:43) Nigel Farage said the donations were "compliant with the law today", but declined to say whether they would be permitted under the proposed changes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-434-0)[\[431\]](#refnote-434)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:43-1)[\[430\]](#refnote-:43)

### Membership

In June 2019, the Brexit Party reportedly had 115,000 paying supporters, though the party did not have an official membership.[&#8202;](#ref-back-435-0)[\[432\]](#refnote-435)

During the week after the 3 June 2024 announcement of Farage's resumption of party leadership, _ITV News_ reported that party membership increased by 50% to 45,000.[&#8202;](#ref-back-436-0)[\[433\]](#refnote-436) By September of that year, the party said it had 80,000 members,[&#8202;](#ref-back-437-0)[\[434\]](#refnote-437) and they hit 100,000 by the end of November.[&#8202;](#ref-back-438-0)[\[435\]](#refnote-438) The party launched an online tracker on 23 December 2024 as it approached overtaking the number of Conservative members; it had 120,549 at noon of that day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-439-0)[\[436\]](#refnote-439) On 26 December, the party said it had surpassed the Conservative party's most recently published total of 131,680, making it the second-largest party by membership.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ref/Con_membership_National-1)[\[83\]](#refnote-Ref/Con_membership_National) On 10 February, the party hit 200,000 members.[&#8202;](#ref-back-440-0)[\[437\]](#refnote-440) During the fallout from the dispute around Rupert Lowe in March, those around Lowe claimed that as many as 7,000 members had resigned in protest, though Reform denied this.[&#8202;](#ref-back-441-0)[\[438\]](#refnote-441) Following an incline to over 230,000 members up to May,[&#8202;](#ref-back-442-0)[\[439\]](#refnote-442) the membership figures have stagnated and dropped slightly since.[&#8202;](#ref-back-443-0)[\[440\]](#refnote-443)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Burqa_membership_drop-0)[\[441\]](#refnote-Burqa_membership_drop)

As of January 2025, the party has 7,800 members in Wales.[&#8202;](#ref-back-445-0)[\[442\]](#refnote-445) In June 2025, the party said it had over 15,000 members in London[&#8202;](#ref-back-446-0)[\[443\]](#refnote-446) and 11,000 members in Scotland.[&#8202;](#ref-back-447-0)[\[444\]](#refnote-447) In Scotland, the party claims to be the third-biggest, beating the Scottish Conservative total of 6,941 in January 2025.[&#8202;](#ref-back-448-0)[\[445\]](#refnote-448)

According to research by [Tim Bale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Bale "Tim Bale") and the _Party Members Project_ done just after the 2024 election, Reform members are similar to those of other parties.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bale-0)[\[446\]](#refnote-Bale) The average age is 61 with very few between 18–24 and almost half of its members are over 65.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bale-1)[\[446\]](#refnote-Bale) Members are predominantly from the middle class, and has a substantial share of its membership in the [Midlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midlands "Midlands") and [North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_England "Northern England").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bale-2)[\[446\]](#refnote-Bale) Nearly all voted leave, and 90% of them consider themselves as being some level of [right-wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-wing_politics "Right-wing politics").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bale-3)[\[446\]](#refnote-Bale)

As of 10June2025[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Reform_UK&action=edit), the membership had dropped from 237,099 to 234,175 in a week. This followed newly elected Reform UK MP [Sarah Pochin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Pochin "Sarah Pochin")'s Commons maiden speech in which she asked if the UK should ban the burqa for public safety as France, Denmark and Belgium had done.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Burqa_membership_drop-1)[\[441\]](#refnote-Burqa_membership_drop)

As of 29August2025[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Reform_UK&action=edit), the party had 237,000 members and 450 branches, up from 80,000 members and no branches at the same time in the previous year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-450-0)[\[447\]](#refnote-450)

As of 12December2025[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Reform_UK&action=edit), Reform UK said it had over 268,000 members, while according to _[The Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times "The Times")_ the Labour Party's membership had fallen to below 250,000. At the time, standard annual membership of Reform cost £25 compared to £70.50 for Labour.[&#8202;](#ref-back-451-0)[\[448\]](#refnote-451)

### Governing board

The six members joining the party leader and chairman to form the inaugural Reform UK Board were announced on 22 August 2025.[&#8202;](#ref-back-452-0)[\[449\]](#refnote-452)
