## Leadership

### Leaders

Main article: [Leader of Reform UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_Reform_UK "Leader of Reform UK")

Reform UK has had three leaders. [Catherine Blaiklock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_Blaiklock "Catherine Blaiklock") was its first leader, in early 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-guard-0)[\[450\]](#refnote-guard) [Nigel Farage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Farage "Nigel Farage") was leader from March 2019 until March 2021, when he resigned and [Richard Tice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Tice "Richard Tice") took on the role.[&#8202;](#ref-back-454-0)[\[451\]](#refnote-454) On 3 June 2024 it was announced that Tice had invited Farage to return as leader, an offer Farage accepted.

### Scotland and Wales

See also: [Leader of Reform UK Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_Reform_UK_Scotland "Leader of Reform UK Scotland") and [Leader of Reform UK Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_Reform_UK_Wales "Leader of Reform UK Wales")

In early 2026, after prolonged vacancies, [Malcolm Offord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_Offord "Malcolm Offord") was appointed leader in Scotland and [Dan Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Thomas "Dan Thomas") was appointed leader in Wales.

## Election results

Main articles: [Brexit Party election results](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brexit_Party_election_results "Brexit Party election results") and [Reform UK election results](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_UK_election_results "Reform UK election results")

### 2019 European Parliament election as the Brexit Party

Main article: [2019 European Parliament election in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election_in_the_United_Kingdom "2019 European Parliament election in the United Kingdom")

The Brexit Party stood candidates in Great Britain at the [2019 European Parliament election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election "2019 European Parliament election"), including the former Conservative Party [Minister of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_State "Minister of State"), [Ann Widdecombe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ann_Widdecombe "Ann Widdecombe"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-457-0)[\[454\]](#refnote-457) the journalist, [Annunziata Rees-Mogg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annunziata_Rees-Mogg "Annunziata Rees-Mogg") (a former Conservative general election candidate and the sister of the Conservative MP and Brexit advocate, [Jacob Rees-Mogg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_Rees-Mogg "Jacob Rees-Mogg")), the [Leave Means Leave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leave_Means_Leave "Leave Means Leave") co-founder, [Richard Tice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Tice "Richard Tice"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-guardian2-1)[\[407\]](#refnote-guardian2) the writers, [Claire Fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claire_Fox "Claire Fox") and [James Heartfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Heartfield "James Heartfield") (both once part of the [Revolutionary Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Communist_Party_(UK,_1978) "Revolutionary Communist Party (UK, 1978)") and later writers for _[Spiked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiked_(magazine) "Spiked (magazine)")_),[&#8202;](#ref-back-458-0)[\[455\]](#refnote-458)[&#8202;](#ref-back-459-0)[\[456\]](#refnote-459) [Stuart Waiton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuart_Waiton "Stuart Waiton") (a fellow _Spiked_ contributor)[&#8202;](#ref-back-460-0)[\[457\]](#refnote-460)[&#8202;](#ref-back-461-0)[\[458\]](#refnote-461) [James Glancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Glancy "James Glancy"), a former member of the [Royal Marines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Marines "Royal Marines") and the [Special Boat Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Boat_Service "Special Boat Service") who was awarded the [Conspicuous Gallantry Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspicuous_Gallantry_Cross "Conspicuous Gallantry Cross"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-462-0)[\[459\]](#refnote-462) [Martin Daubney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Daubney "Martin Daubney"), a journalist and commentator,[&#8202;](#ref-back-463-0)[\[460\]](#refnote-463) [David Bull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Bull_(politician) "David Bull (politician)"), author and television presenter,[&#8202;](#ref-back-464-0)[\[461\]](#refnote-464)[&#8202;](#ref-back-465-0)[\[462\]](#refnote-465) [Brian Monteith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Monteith "Brian Monteith"), a former Conservative Party [MSP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_of_the_Scottish_Parliament "Member of the Scottish Parliament"), [Rupert Lowe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert_Lowe "Rupert Lowe"), a businessman[&#8202;](#ref-back-466-0)[\[463\]](#refnote-466) and retired Rear Admiral [Roger Lane-Nott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Lane-Nott "Roger Lane-Nott").[&#8202;](#ref-back-467-0)[\[464\]](#refnote-467) [John Longworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Longworth_(businessman) "John Longworth (businessman)"), the former director-general of the British Chambers of Commerce, announced he would be standing as a candidate for the party on 15 April 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-468-0)[\[465\]](#refnote-468) The party was not registered in Northern Ireland and did not field candidates there.[&#8202;](#ref-back-469-0)[\[466\]](#refnote-469)

A survey of 781 Conservative Party councillors found that 40% planned to vote for the Brexit Party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-470-0)[\[467\]](#refnote-470) On 17 April 2019, the former Labour and [Respect Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respect_Party "Respect Party") MP [George Galloway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Galloway "George Galloway") announced his support for the Brexit Party "for one-time only" in the 2019 European Parliament election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-471-0)[\[468\]](#refnote-471) On 24 April, the political columnist [Tim Montgomerie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Montgomerie "Tim Montgomerie") announced that he would vote for the party and endorsed Widdecombe's candidature,[&#8202;](#ref-back-472-0)[\[469\]](#refnote-472) and the Conservative MP [Lucy Allan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucy_Allan_(politician) "Lucy Allan (politician)") described the candidates of the party as "fantastic".[&#8202;](#ref-back-473-0)[\[470\]](#refnote-473) On 2 May, one of the party's candidates for the North West constituency, Sally Bate, resigned from the party in response to previous comments made by Claire Fox, the lead candidate in the constituency, on the [Warrington bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warrington_bombings "Warrington bombings").[&#8202;](#ref-back-474-0)[\[471\]](#refnote-474)

In May 2019, several polls forecast the party polling first for the European elections,[&#8202;](#ref-back-475-0)[\[472\]](#refnote-475) though earlier polls had suggested it would come third to Labour and the Conservatives.[&#8202;](#ref-back-476-0)[\[473\]](#refnote-476) The party held 14 seats, acquired through defections, going into the elections, and saw an increase of 15. It won five more seats than UKIP, had at the [previous election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_European_Parliament_election_in_the_United_Kingdom "2014 European Parliament election in the United Kingdom"), under Farage's leadership.

#### Results

The party won 29 seats in the [election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election_in_the_United_Kingdom "2019 European Parliament election in the United Kingdom"), becoming the biggest single party in the [Ninth European Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninth_European_Parliament "Ninth European Parliament"). The [CDU/CSU Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CDU/CSU "CDU/CSU") also won 29 seats in [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election_in_Germany "2019 European Parliament election in Germany"), but it was an alliance and not a party. Three of the 29 resigned the whip in December 2019 to support the [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK) "Conservative Party (UK)") at the [2019 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election "2019 United Kingdom general election"), while a fourth, [John Longworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Longworth_(businessman) "John Longworth (businessman)"), was expelled for "repeatedly undermining" the party's election strategy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-477-0)[\[474\]](#refnote-477)

### 2019 general election

Main article: [2019 United Kingdom general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election "2019 United Kingdom general election")

On 19 April, Farage said that the party intended to stand candidates at the [2019 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election "2019 United Kingdom general election"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-478-0)[\[475\]](#refnote-478) but would not stand candidates against the 28 Eurosceptic Conservative MPs who opposed the [Brexit withdrawal agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brexit_withdrawal_agreement "Brexit withdrawal agreement").[&#8202;](#ref-back-479-0)[\[476\]](#refnote-479) In the [Peterborough by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Peterborough_by-election "2019 Peterborough by-election") in June, the Brexit Party came second with 28% of the vote, 7% ahead of the Conservatives and 2% behind Labour.

Following [Boris Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Johnson "Boris Johnson")'s election as Prime Minister, Farage unveiled the names of 635 general election candidates for the Brexit Party, including himself.[&#8202;](#ref-back-480-0)[\[477\]](#refnote-480) On 8 September 2019, Farage wrote an article in the _[The Sunday Telegraph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sunday_Telegraph "The Sunday Telegraph")_ and the party took out advertisements in Sunday newspapers offering an electoral pact with the Conservative Party in the forthcoming general election, whereby the Brexit Party would not be opposed by the Conservatives in traditional Labour Party seats in the north of England, the Midlands and Wales, and the Brexit Party would not contest seats in which they could split the Leave vote. Farage wrote that Boris Johnson should ask himself "does he want to sign a non-aggression pact with me and return to Downing Street?"[&#8202;](#ref-back-481-0)[\[478\]](#refnote-481)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Brexit_Party_seats_contested_in_the_2019_GE.svg/500px-Brexit_Party_seats_contested_in_the_2019_GE.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Brexit_Party_seats_contested_in_the_2019_GE.svg) Constituencies, highlighted, which the Brexit Party contested at the [2019 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election "2019 United Kingdom general election")

Farage's proposition was rejected by Johnson.[&#8202;](#ref-back-482-0)[\[479\]](#refnote-482) On 11 November, Farage said that his party would not stand in any of the 317 seats won by the Conservatives at the last election. Conservative Party chairman [James Cleverly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cleverly "James Cleverly") welcomed this, although he stated that the parties had not been in contact.[&#8202;](#ref-back-483-0)[\[480\]](#refnote-483) _[Newsnight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newsnight "Newsnight")_ reported that conversations between members of the Brexit Party and the pro-Brexit Conservative group, the [European Research Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Research_Group "European Research Group") (ERG) had led to this decision.[&#8202;](#ref-back-newsnight11-0)[\[481\]](#refnote-newsnight11)

The Brexit Party is reported to have requested that Boris Johnson publicly state he would not extend the Brexit transition period beyond the planned date of 31 December 2020 and that he wished for a Canada-style free-trade agreement with the EU. Johnson did make a statement covering these two issues, something which Farage referenced as key when announcing he was standing down some candidates, but both the Brexit Party and the Conservatives denied that any deal was done between them.[&#8202;](#ref-back-newsnight11-1)[\[481\]](#refnote-newsnight11)[&#8202;](#ref-back-485-0)[\[482\]](#refnote-485)[&#8202;](#ref-back-486-0)[\[483\]](#refnote-486) The decision to not run in those seats met with criticism by some Brexit Party supporters and candidates, and some candidates who had been selected to run for [Conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK) "Conservative Party (UK)") seats opted to run as [independent candidates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_politician "Independent politician") on a [Pro-Brexit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brexit "Brexit") platform.[&#8202;](#ref-back-487-0)[\[484\]](#refnote-487)

#### Results

The party failed to win any seats in the general election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-killed-1)[\[26\]](#refnote-killed) Its best second places were in [Barnsley Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barnsley_Central "Barnsley Central"), where Victoria Felton won 30.4% of the vote,[&#8202;](#ref-back-488-0)[\[485\]](#refnote-488) and [Barnsley East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barnsley_East "Barnsley East"), where Jim Ferguson won 29.2%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-489-0)[\[486\]](#refnote-489) High third places were in [Hartlepool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hartlepool_(constituency) "Hartlepool (constituency)"), where Richard Tice won 25.8% of the vote,[&#8202;](#ref-back-hull-0)[\[487\]](#refnote-hull) and [Kingston upon Hull West and Hessle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingston_upon_Hull_West_and_Hessle "Kingston upon Hull West and Hessle"), where [Michelle Dewberry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Dewberry "Michelle Dewberry") won 18%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-hull-1)[\[487\]](#refnote-hull)

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election "2019 United Kingdom general election") | [Nigel Farage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Farage "Nigel Farage") | 644,257 | 2.01 | 0 / 650 | 0% | Extra-parliamentary |

### 2024 general election

Main article: [2024 United Kingdom general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election "2024 United Kingdom general election")

On 22 May 2024, Prime Minister [Rishi Sunak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rishi_Sunak "Rishi Sunak") announced the date of the [general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election "2024 United Kingdom general election") as 4 July. The next day, Tice launched the Reform UK campaign, promising to field candidates in 630 seats including himself in [Boston and Skegness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_and_Skegness "Boston and Skegness").[&#8202;](#ref-back-491-0)[\[488\]](#refnote-491) He said the party wanted to make this the "immigration election".[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc-rules-out-1)[\[49\]](#refnote-bbc-rules-out) Farage initially ruled out standing, saying it was "not the right time" but promised to "do my bit to help".[&#8202;](#ref-back-guardian-u-turn-1)[\[61\]](#refnote-guardian-u-turn) In the first week of the campaign, Reform's predicted vote in opinion polls rose from 11% to 13%, although many commentators predicted their share would be squeezed[&#8202;](#ref-back-492-0)[\[489\]](#refnote-492) and the Conservatives announced policies targeted at Reform voters, such as [national service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_service "National service").[&#8202;](#ref-back-493-0)[\[490\]](#refnote-493)

On 3 June, Farage became the leader of Reform UK, and pollsters reported an increase in support for the party, in two cases polling within 2% of the Conservative Party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-494-0)[\[491\]](#refnote-494) BBC analyst Peter Barnes commented on 9 June that the change in leadership "has clearly had a positive impact on the party's performance in the polls", and that this "has come at the expense of the Conservatives".[&#8202;](#ref-back-495-0)[\[492\]](#refnote-495) A poll conducted after the BBC's seven-party debate held on 7 June found Farage to be the winner with 25% support, his closest rival being Labour Party deputy leader [Angela Rayner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Rayner "Angela Rayner"), on 19%. The debate majored on the [Normandy landings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normandy_landings "Normandy landings"), war veterans, immigration and the [National Health Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Health_Service "National Health Service") (NHS).[&#8202;](#ref-back-496-0)[\[493\]](#refnote-496)

Farage said that his aim was to make Reform the [Official Opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/His_Majesty's_Most_Loyal_Opposition "His Majesty's Most Loyal Opposition") party in Parliament.[&#8202;](#ref-back-497-0)[\[494\]](#refnote-497) Reform would be standing in 609 out of 650 constituencies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-498-0)[\[495\]](#refnote-498) As part of an electoral pact with the [Social Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_(UK,_1990%E2%80%93present) "Social Democratic Party (UK, 1990–present)"), the two parties stood aside from each other in six constituencies and a dozen candidates stood under a joint Reform-SDP banner.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SDP_Pact-0)[\[496\]](#refnote-SDP_Pact)

On 10 June, the Reform UK candidate for [Bexhill and Battle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bexhill_and_Battle "Bexhill and Battle"), Ian Gribbin, was reported as having said in 2022 that: "Britain would be in a far better state today had we taken Hitler up on his offer of neutrality." Gribbin apologised without reservation for the comment and upset caused.[&#8202;](#ref-back-500-0)[\[497\]](#refnote-500) A party spokesman defended Gribbin by saying that "his historical perspective of what the UK could have done in the 30s was shared by the vast majority of the British establishment including the BBC of its day, and is probably true" that the comments made by Gribbin were not endorsements of the stances and that the party would continue to support him.[&#8202;](#ref-back-501-0)[\[498\]](#refnote-501)[&#8202;](#ref-back-502-0)[\[499\]](#refnote-502) _The Times_ reported on 13 June that 41 of Reform candidates were [Facebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook "Facebook") friends with the British [neo-fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-fascism "Neo-fascism") leader [Gary Raikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Raikes "Gary Raikes").[&#8202;](#ref-back-503-0)[\[500\]](#refnote-503) After revelations about the candidates,[&#8202;](#ref-back-tns001-0)[\[501\]](#refnote-tns001) Farage said on 18 June that the party had hired a vetting company, but had been "stitched up" by them. The company, vetting.com, responded that there had been insufficient time to complete their work, the election having been called earlier than expected.[&#8202;](#ref-back-tns001-1)[\[501\]](#refnote-tns001)

In the campaign, the party used the slogan "Britain Needs Reform".[&#8202;](#ref-back-505-0)[\[502\]](#refnote-505) Its party election video, broadcast nationally on 13 June, showed silently and continuously for 4 minutes and 40 seconds the six words "Britain is Broken. Britain Needs Reform".[&#8202;](#ref-back-506-0)[\[503\]](#refnote-506) On 13 June, [YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov "YouGov") polling put Reform at 19% and the Conservatives 18%. Farage declared "We are now the opposition to Labour."[&#8202;](#ref-back-507-0)[\[504\]](#refnote-507) On 15 June, the BBC's [Laura Kuenssberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laura_Kuenssberg "Laura Kuenssberg") said that "the most optimistic Reform politicians can't name more than five or six seats where they reckon they could win."[&#8202;](#ref-back-508-0)[\[505\]](#refnote-508) Pollsters Survation published the results of a survey of 42,269 voters employing [multilevel regression with poststratification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multilevel_regression_with_poststratification "Multilevel regression with poststratification") (MRP) which predicted that Reform would win seven seats and YouGov's MRP survey predicted five seats.[&#8202;](#ref-back-PoliticoYou-0)[\[506\]](#refnote-PoliticoYou)

On 20 June, the BBC reported that while Farage has been criticised by some Muslim organisations for saying that a growing number of young Muslims do not subscribe to British values, Muslim entrepreneur [Zia Yusuf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zia_Yusuf "Zia Yusuf") had just given the party a donation amounting to hundreds of thousands of pounds and said that the country has lost control of its borders. He said that it was his "patriotic duty" to fund Farage and Reform UK.[&#8202;](#ref-back-510-0)[\[507\]](#refnote-510)

Farage was criticised during the campaign for suggesting that the West had provoked Russia's invasion of Ukraine by expanding the EU and [NATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO "NATO") military alliance eastwards. Farage also said that "of course" the war was the fault of Putin.[&#8202;](#ref-back-511-0)[\[508\]](#refnote-511)

On 27 June, _[Channel 4 News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_4_News "Channel 4 News")_ revealed homophobic, racist and Islamophobic comments made by party campaigners in [Clacton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clacton_(constituency) "Clacton (constituency)"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-512-0)[\[509\]](#refnote-512)[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBC_News-0)[\[510\]](#refnote-BBC_News) including an individual calling Sunak a " [Paki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paki "Paki")" (a racist slur against those of South Asian heritage),[&#8202;](#ref-back-smh-0)[\[511\]](#refnote-smh) and suggesting the army should shoot at [boats bringing illegal migrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Channel_illegal_migrant_crossings_(2018%E2%80%93present) "English Channel illegal migrant crossings (2018–present)") to the UK,[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBC_News-1)[\[510\]](#refnote-BBC_News) and another campaigner calling the [LGBT flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_flag "LGBT flag") "degenerate". Sunak responded that hearing the slur against him "hurts and it makes me angry".[&#8202;](#ref-back-AP-racist-0)[\[512\]](#refnote-AP-racist)[&#8202;](#ref-back-nyt-0)[\[513\]](#refnote-nyt)[&#8202;](#ref-back-517-0)[\[514\]](#refnote-517) Farage described the anti-gay comments as "vulgar, drunk and wrong"[&#8202;](#ref-back-518-0)[\[515\]](#refnote-518) and condemned the racist comments, before suggesting the programme was a "set up" by Channel 4, as the individual who made the racist slur against Sunak, Andrew Parker, was an actor and that it alluded to foul play.[&#8202;](#ref-back-AP-racist-1)[\[512\]](#refnote-AP-racist) The party said it made a complaint against Channel 4 for "electoral interference",[&#8202;](#ref-back-519-0)[\[516\]](#refnote-519) although reports on 28 June suggested the [Electoral Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Commission_(United_Kingdom) "Electoral Commission (United Kingdom)") had not received such a complaint.[&#8202;](#ref-back-lbc-0)[\[517\]](#refnote-lbc) Channel 4 commented: "We met Mr Parker for the first time at Reform UK party headquarters, where he was a Reform party canvasser. We did not pay the Reform UK canvasser or anyone else in this report. Mr Parker was not known to Channel 4 News and was filmed covertly via the undercover operation."[&#8202;](#ref-back-521-0)[\[518\]](#refnote-521) Following the report, Reform UK dropped its support for three candidates because of past racist comments,[&#8202;](#ref-back-lbc-1)[\[517\]](#refnote-lbc) and on 30 June, one candidate defected to the Conservatives over a perceived lack of leadership from Reform on the issue.[&#8202;](#ref-back-522-0)[\[519\]](#refnote-522)[&#8202;](#ref-back-523-0)[\[520\]](#refnote-523)

Less than 20% of Reform candidates were women and the five elected were all men and had a median age of 60.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Barker-Singh-0)[\[521\]](#refnote-Barker-Singh)

#### Results

The party won five seats ( [Ashfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashfield_(constituency) "Ashfield (constituency)"), [Clacton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clacton_(constituency) "Clacton (constituency)"), [Boston and Skegness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_and_Skegness "Boston and Skegness"), [Great Yarmouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Yarmouth_(constituency) "Great Yarmouth (constituency)"), and [South Basildon and East Thurrock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Basildon_and_East_Thurrock "South Basildon and East Thurrock")) and came second in 98.[&#8202;](#ref-back-525-0)[\[522\]](#refnote-525) Reform's presence split the right-wing vote, allowing Labour to win seats on small margins including [South West Norfolk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_West_Norfolk "South West Norfolk"), [Poole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poole_(UK_Parliament_constituency) "Poole (UK Parliament constituency)"), [South Dorset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Dorset "South Dorset") and [Rother Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rother_Valley_(UK_Parliament_constituency) "Rother Valley (UK Parliament constituency)"). The party won 14% of the vote in total.[&#8202;](#ref-back-meet-1)[\[62\]](#refnote-meet) It became the third-largest party by popular vote, gaining 4,117,610 votes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-526-0)[\[523\]](#refnote-526)

Almost 80% of people who voted for Reform had voted for the Conservatives in the previous 2019 election, so 25% of the people who voted for the Conservatives in 2019 switched their vote to Reform in 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-527-0)[\[524\]](#refnote-527):92–93

| No. | Share | Seats won | ± |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| [2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election "2024 United Kingdom general election") | [Nigel Farage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Farage "Nigel Farage") | 4,117,610 | 14.3% | 5 / 650 | ![Increase](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Increase2.svg/40px-Increase2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) 5 | Opposition |

### Local government

The party first stood at local government level in two by-elections in [Gloucester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloucester "Gloucester") on 25 July 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-528-0)[\[525\]](#refnote-528) They did not win either.[&#8202;](#ref-back-529-0)[\[526\]](#refnote-529)

A councillor elected to [Rochdale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rochdale_Borough_Council "Rochdale Borough Council") defected to the party in July 2019 from Labour, making for the first councillor;[&#8202;](#ref-back-530-0)[\[527\]](#refnote-530)[&#8202;](#ref-back-531-0)[\[528\]](#refnote-531) shortly after a [Liberal Democrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Democrats_(UK) "Liberal Democrats (UK)") councillor there also defected.[&#8202;](#ref-back-532-0)[\[529\]](#refnote-532) All 12 of [Rotherham's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotherham_Metropolitan_Borough_Council_elections "Rotherham Metropolitan Borough Council elections") then UKIP councillors defected to the Brexit Party in July 2019, as did all 5 of [Derby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derby_City_Council "Derby City Council")'s UKIP councillors.[&#8202;](#ref-back-533-0)[\[530\]](#refnote-533)[&#8202;](#ref-back-534-0)[\[531\]](#refnote-534) On 13 September 2019, ten independent councillors on [Hartlepool Borough Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hartlepool_Borough_Council "Hartlepool Borough Council") defected to the Brexit Party. They then formed a pact with the three Conservatives to hold 13 of the 33 seats.[&#8202;](#ref-back-535-0)[\[532\]](#refnote-535) In September 2019, a Conservative councillor for [Surrey (county)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrey_County_Council "Surrey County Council") and [Elmbridge (borough)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elmbridge_Borough_Council "Elmbridge Borough Council") defected to the party, after his party decided he would not be reselected.[&#8202;](#ref-back-536-0)[\[533\]](#refnote-536)

The 13 councillors of the Hartlepool council group left the party in 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-537-0)[\[534\]](#refnote-537) The Rotherham group left to form the Rotherham Democratic Party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-538-0)[\[535\]](#refnote-538)[&#8202;](#ref-back-539-0)[\[536\]](#refnote-539) The party won two seats in the [2021 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_Kingdom_local_elections "2021 United Kingdom local elections"), both in [Derby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Derby_City_Council_election "2021 Derby City Council election"), one a hold from a previous defection and the other a gain. These were the first council seats won at election by the party, as all their previous ones had been via defections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-540-0)[\[537\]](#refnote-540)[&#8202;](#ref-back-541-0)[\[538\]](#refnote-541) This left them with eight councillors in total; six in Derby and two more from defections, one in [Redbridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redbridge_London_Borough_Council "Redbridge London Borough Council") from the Conservatives, and one in [Swale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borough_of_Swale "Borough of Swale") from UKIP, both in April 2021.[&#8202;](#ref-back-542-0)[\[539\]](#refnote-542)[&#8202;](#ref-back-543-0)[\[540\]](#refnote-543) Councillors in the Derby City group are members of an affiliate party named " [Reform Derby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Derby "Reform Derby")", in alignment with Reform UK.[&#8202;](#ref-back-544-0)[\[541\]](#refnote-544)[&#8202;](#ref-back-545-0)[\[542\]](#refnote-545)

In December 2021, days before the [North Shropshire by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_North_Shropshire_by-election "2021 North Shropshire by-election"), local councillor and Deputy Mayor of [Market Drayton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_Drayton "Market Drayton") Town Council, Mark Whittle, defected to the party from the Conservatives.[&#8202;](#ref-back-546-0)[\[543\]](#refnote-546) It was reported that all of Reform UK's candidates in the [2022 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_Kingdom_local_elections "2022 United Kingdom local elections") "will campaign on the benefits of [fracking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fracking "Fracking") and restarting exploration in the North Sea".[&#8202;](#ref-back-tele-new-brexit-1)[\[350\]](#refnote-tele-new-brexit) Three of the eight council seats held by the party were up for re-election in 2022, all of which had arisen from defections. Both [Derby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Derby_City_Council_election "2022 Derby City Council election") seats were held, but a seat in Redbridge was lost. No new seats were gained.[&#8202;](#ref-back-latest-0)[\[544\]](#refnote-latest)

In December 2022, two former Conservative councillors – one in [Barnsley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barnsley_Metropolitan_Borough_Council "Barnsley Metropolitan Borough Council") and the other in [West Oxfordshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Oxfordshire "West Oxfordshire") – defected to the party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-548-0)[\[545\]](#refnote-548) Another Conservative councillor, Barry Gwilt, of the Fazeley ward of [Lichfield District Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lichfield_District#Political_control "Lichfield District"), defected to Reform UK in January 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-549-0)[\[546\]](#refnote-549) In the [2023 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_United_Kingdom_local_elections "2023 United Kingdom local elections"), Reform UK won six seats out of the 8,519 up for election[&#8202;](#ref-back-550-0)[\[547\]](#refnote-550) and averaged 6% of the vote in the wards where it stood.[&#8202;](#ref-back-551-0)[\[548\]](#refnote-551) The six seats won were all in the City of Derby, whose new council proceeded to elect Reform Derby leader Alan Graves to the position of Mayor for 2023/24.[&#8202;](#ref-back-552-0)[\[549\]](#refnote-552)

In March 2024, [East Riding of Yorkshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Riding_of_Yorkshire "East Riding of Yorkshire") councillor Maria Bowtell defected from the Conservatives and joined the party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-553-0)[\[550\]](#refnote-553) In the [2024 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_local_elections "2024 United Kingdom local elections"), Reform UK took approximately 11% of the vote where it stood candidates,[&#8202;](#ref-back-554-0)[\[551\]](#refnote-554) and won two seats on [Havant Borough Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borough_of_Havant "Borough of Havant")[&#8202;](#ref-back-555-0)[\[552\]](#refnote-555) and one on the London Assembly.[&#8202;](#ref-back-London_Elects_2024-2)[\[60\]](#refnote-London_Elects_2024) Richard Tice said that his party was becoming the real opposition to Labour.[&#8202;](#ref-back-556-0)[\[553\]](#refnote-556) On 18 June, four Conservatives from the [Tendring District Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tendring_District#Political_control "Tendring District") defected to Reform, with Jeff Bray becoming leader of the council group.[&#8202;](#ref-back-557-0)[\[554\]](#refnote-557)

Since the 2024 general election, Reform UK has won a number of council by-elections. Thirty-two councils now have at least one Reform UK councillor,[&#8202;](#ref-back-558-0)[\[555\]](#refnote-558) with the party winning by-elections in [Blackpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackpool_Council "Blackpool Council"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-559-0)[\[556\]](#refnote-559) [Dartford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borough_of_Dartford "Borough of Dartford"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-560-0)[\[557\]](#refnote-560) [East Riding of Yorkshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Riding_of_Yorkshire_Council "East Riding of Yorkshire Council"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-561-0)[\[558\]](#refnote-561) [Kent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kent_County_Council "Kent County Council"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-562-0)[\[559\]](#refnote-562) [St Helens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Helens_Borough_Council "St Helens Borough Council"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-563-0)[\[560\]](#refnote-563) [Swale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borough_of_Swale "Borough of Swale"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-564-0)[\[561\]](#refnote-564) [Wolverhampton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Wolverhampton_Council "City of Wolverhampton Council"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-565-0)[\[562\]](#refnote-565) and [Wyre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borough_of_Wyre "Borough of Wyre").[&#8202;](#ref-back-566-0)[\[563\]](#refnote-566) On 10 January 2025, ten Reform councillors resigned from the party, saying that the party is being run in an "increasingly autocratic manner" since Farage's return as the party's leader.[&#8202;](#ref-back-JC-0)[\[564\]](#refnote-JC) On 14 February 2025, Stuart Keyte became the first elected councillor for Reform UK in Wales, joining three other Reform councillors at Torfaen Council, who had defected to the party after previously sitting as independents.[&#8202;](#ref-back-568-0)[\[565\]](#refnote-568)

In March 2025, Reform UK gained defecting councillors in Scotland. John Gray from [Renfrewshire Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renfrewshire_Council "Renfrewshire Council") and Ross Lambie from [South Lanarkshire Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Lanarkshire_Council "South Lanarkshire Council") both defected from the Conservative Party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-569-0)[\[566\]](#refnote-569) On 11 March 2025, [Falkirk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falkirk_(council_area) "Falkirk (council area)") councillor Claire Mackie-Brown also joined Reform UK from the Conservative Party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-570-0)[\[567\]](#refnote-570) Farage welcomed 29 defecting councillors at a press conference in [Westminster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster "Westminster"). Of Reform UK's 113 council seats, 98 have come about via defections from politicians that were elected for another party – the majority, 66 from the Conservative Party – while 15 have been won through elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc-c871wzx8r47o-2)[\[94\]](#refnote-bbc-c871wzx8r47o) Amid this the Councillor Maria Bowtell left the party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-571-0)[\[568\]](#refnote-571)

At the [2025 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_United_Kingdom_local_elections "2025 United Kingdom local elections"), Reform stood 1,706 candidates[&#8202;](#ref-back-572-0)[\[569\]](#refnote-572) representing 97.5% of all wards up for election. It went on to win 677 seats and a majority of seats on 10 councils. The party also won 2 of the 6 mayoral elections taking place, [Greater Lincolnshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Greater_Lincolnshire_mayoral_election "2025 Greater Lincolnshire mayoral election") and [Hull and East Yorkshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Hull_and_East_Yorkshire_mayoral_election "2025 Hull and East Yorkshire mayoral election"). A projected national vote share collated by the [BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC "BBC") put Reform on 30% of the vote slightly ahead of its position in opinion polls conducted immediately prior to the local elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-573-0)[\[570\]](#refnote-573) After four of Reform's new councillors stepped down, the party were unable to retain three of those seats in the subsequent by-elections, losing two to the Conservatives and the other to the Liberal Democrats.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc-conservative-win-1)[\[251\]](#refnote-bbc-conservative-win)[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc-durham-1)[\[250\]](#refnote-bbc-durham)[&#8202;](#ref-back-durham-bye-1)[\[253\]](#refnote-durham-bye)

Data has shown that most of Reform UK's representation comes from those who have defected from the Conservative Party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Barker-Singh-1)[\[521\]](#refnote-Barker-Singh)

At the [2026 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_Kingdom_local_elections "2026 United Kingdom local elections"), Reform stood 4,821 candidates representing 95% of all wards up for election. It went on to win 1,453 seats and a majority of seats on 14 councils.

## Notes

1. [&#8202;](#refnote-129)[↑](#ref-back-129-0)Although [Lee Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Anderson "Lee Anderson"), the [MP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(United_Kingdom) "Member of Parliament (United Kingdom)") for [Ashfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashfield_(UK_Parliament_constituency) "Ashfield (UK Parliament constituency)"), defected to Reform UK in March 2024 becoming their first MP after formerly being of the [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK) "Conservative Party (UK)"), at the time of his defection he was an independent after having the Conservative whip suspended and being kicked out of the party in February 2024. Danny Kruger, therefore, was the first MP to defect to Reform UK from another party and not as an independent.
2. [&#8202;](#refnote-populist)[↑](#ref-back-populist-0)Also described as simply [populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populist "Populist")[&#8202;](#ref-back-270-0)[\[269\]](#refnote-270) as well as populist radical right[&#8202;](#ref-back-f561-0)[\[270\]](#refnote-f561)
3. [&#8202;](#refnote-hard-right)[↑](#ref-back-hard-right-0)Also described by media as [hard-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-wing_politics "Right-wing politics")[&#8202;](#ref-back-276-0)[\[274\]](#refnote-276)[&#8202;](#ref-back-277-0)[\[275\]](#refnote-277)[&#8202;](#ref-back-278-0)[\[276\]](#refnote-278)
